Man of Peace
Flavius Aetius was a patrician of Rome who led troops into victory against the Huns, the Franks, the Burgundians, and the Goths, and has often been called "the Last of the Romans".
Aetius was a tragic hero during the twilight years of the western Roman Empire. He supported and served the Empire through 20 years of crisis. Over two decades, he suppressed rebellions in Gaul, destroyed the kingdom established by the Burgundians in present-day Worms, and beat the Visigoths in Toulouse before allying with them to defeat Attila at the Battle of the Catalaunian Plains.
Thanks to his efforts, the people of the Western Roman Empire enjoyed a period of relative security, but despite this, he was unable to prevent its eventual collapse. In 454 CE, Aetius met with Emperor Valentinian. Following an argument, Valentinian drew his sword and killed him. Aetius did not die a hero's death on the battlefield, but instead fell to the blade of a cowardly emperor.
Scipio Africanus was a Roman general and statesman, born to an esteemed noble family. His family had produced a number of Roman consuls, and he had seized Spain for the Roman Empire in his youth, driving out the Carthaginians.
The peak of Scipio's military career was during the Battle of Zama in 202 BCE. Through judicious use of tactics, he neutralized the threat of Carthaginian war elephants and broke through Hannibal's formation, exposing the Carthaginian army's flanks and laying the groundwork for a Roman victory. Though Scipio's armored infantry were lacking in mobility, their ingenious use of formation allowed them to defeat the Carthaginians.
By this point, Scipio had driven the Carthaginians out of Spain as well as from their own lands, defeated the "Father of Tactics" Hannibal Barca, and forced the Carthaginians into a peace agreement with Rome, bringing victory to the Romans in the Second Punic War.C
Thutmose III was a renowned military leader and pharaoh of the Eighteenth Dynasty of ancient Egypt, most well known for his military prowess. After officially beginning his reign in 1458 BCE, he began building his empire, progressively expanding his rule to include Syria and Palestine. In 1445 BCE, Thutmose defeated the king of Mitanni in battle, gaining control over the west bank of the Euphrates. Under his military leadership, Egypt grew into an empire that spanned land and sea. Thutmose's military accomplishments gained him the title of "the Napoleon of ancient Egypt".
Throughout his entire life, Thutmose was never defeated in battle. Of all the Egyptian dynasties, the Eighteenth Dynasty ruled over the largest territory, had the strongest armies, and lasted longest, and Thutmose III undoubtedly played a critical role in this accomplishment. During his reign, the rulers of Libya and Babylon paid tribute to him, and Palestine and Syria became part of Egyptian territory. He was a brave and highly skilled military leader who led Egypt into an era of unprecedented prosperity.3
Imhotep was chancellor to the pharaoh Djoser during the Third Dynasty of ancient Egypt, and was High Priest of the sun god Ra in Heliopolis. His status did not come as a result of noble birth
he was born a commoner
but rather as a result of his scholarship and wisdom which allowed him to rise through the ranks and become the pharaoh's right-hand man.
Djoser recognized Imhotep's talent, and Imhotep repaid the pharaoh for his appreciation. Djoser once had a dream in which he ascended a staircase into the heavens. Thus, Imhotep built the step pyramid, to allow the pharaoh's soul to return to the bosom of the sun god Ra. The step pyramid is considered the first man-made structure to be made completely from stone.
Imhotep's talents were not only used in the service of the royal family. As the pharaoh's physician, he built the first ever academy for the study of medicine, so that he could pass on his medical knowledge for the good of the masses. In an era lacking in medical skills, Imhotep treated over two hundred types of illness, and is considered the founder of ancient Egyptian medicine. After his death, he came to be revered as the god of medicine, and as the son of the god Ptah. Imhotep's knowledge allowed him to rise from a commoner to a god, and made sure that his name would be known throughout history.)

Henry V (1386-1422) was King of England and a member of the House of Lancaster. Though he only reigned for nine years, he achieved much on the field of battle. During his reign, the Hundred Years' War between England and France reignited; Henry took advantage of internal struggles within France, maintaining an alliance with the Duke of Burgundy to England's great advantage. In 1415, he formally invaded France, occupying French territory with an army of longbowmen. In 1419, Henry conquered Normandy, compelling the French king Charles VI to sign the Treaty of Troyes and winning a huge victory for England in the Hundred Years' War. Despite his substantial achievements, Henry passed away from illness in 1422.
Alongside his political and military talents, Henry also had great personal charisma. He was praised for administering justice, becoming known as an impartial and incorruptible judge. He was also known for his love of art and music, and could play the Welsh harp and the flute. He was an ardent Christian, and forbade his soldiers from ransacking churches during his conquest of France.
Boudica was queen of an ancient Celtic tribe known as the Iceni.
After the death of her husband Prasutagus, the Romans plundered Iceni lands, levying heavy taxes, flogging Boudica and violating her daughters. Enraged by this injustice, Boudica led an army of British tribes in an uprising against the Roman Empire.
Boudica's army first headed toward Camulodunum (modern-day Colchester), where they defeated the weak Roman garrison and sacked the city, destroying a temple to the former Emperor Claudius. The Roman Ninth Legion attempted to save the city, but suffered an overwhelming defeat. Boudica's army slaughtered, sacrificed, and feasted among the ashes of the city they had conquered, proclaiming the beginning of her revenge against Rome.
Stormbringer
Wolfshadow
Second son to the Permanent Chief's family, a prince of his people but no future king, Torgny had remained a relatively little known presence on Ceroli Islands before the outbreak of the Crisis, and yet the seeds of his eventual notoriety had been sown much earlier by his ruthless ambition and envy towards his elder brother, Garlo. Torgny wasted no effort in building a strong powerbase and sowing such divisions and questions of legitimacy as he could in over two decades garrisoning the northern ranges.
If there was one spark that lit the coup's match it was rumors of Logar's involvement in forbidden heresy. Torgny's elite troops quickly surrounded the Citadel of Braves and his coronation as the new "Permanent Chief" came not long after.
Wafura's clan must be one of the most mysterious tribes on this continent, subsisting as they do on this barren, freezing desert on which hardly anything grows or can grow. Miles of dunes lie in every direction, confusing adventurers unfortunate enough to brave this wintry country, broken only by the occasional dust storms by which Wafura's artful clansfolk conceal themselves: the true communal savages and masters of the desert.
The desert has taught Wafura to be as merciless and cruel as the wolf, and yet there is no denying the simultaneous mix of hatred and envy the savage feel for the wealthier, better-placed Ceroli royals. Wafura talked much with the usurper Torgny prior to the outbreak of the Ceroli Crisis - if only to keep tabs on the situation and encourage further chaos. Now the Crisis has upended the existing order, Wafura stands ready by the gates to his desert to lead his "wolfpack" on new, bloody adventures...

Jan 
ka of the Chalice
Jan 
ka was a famous military commander and innovator who was a national hero of Bohemia. He was born to a relatively poor noble family, and lost an eye in his youth, leading him to be nicknamed "One-Eyed 
ka". At a young age, 
ka became an accomplished mercenary, fighting only for his own profit. But hearing a sermon by the theologian and reformer Jan Hus changed his life, and he became a defender of Hus and a leader of his armies.
ka's military prowess was undeniable. He wiped out the opposing forces of the Teutonic Knights during the Battle of Grunwald, fought in the civil war against the King of Bohemia, and put an end to the First Anti-Hussite Crusade at the Battle of Vitkov Hill. He recruited a force of inexperienced farmers and trained them into a well-disciplined army. He also developed famous wagon formations that were almost impenetrable. 
ka never lost a battle in his life, and his feats led to him being voted one of the greatest Czechs in history.

Joan of Arc
Virgin Warrior
Joan of Arc, also known as Saint Joan, was the daughter of a French peasant family, born during the Hundred Years' War between the French and the English. At the time, internal struggles within France had led to a string of battlefield defeats against the English invaders, who had occupied northern France, making life hard for its people.
At a time of great strife in France, Joan encountered a miracle. Following the "voice of God", she joined the expedition to save the city of Orl
ans. On the battlefield, she broke with the French army's careful strategy, leading an all-out assault. Under her leadership, the French army encircled Orleans and achieved victory in one of the major turning points of the Hundred Years' War. She went on to lead the French to further victories.
In 1920, Joan of Arc was made a saint. An unyielding heroine, this young woman became one of the most important cultural symbols in France and the West, with many writers and composers creating works about her.

Enlil's Blessings6
Cavalry March Speed Bonus (Outside Alliance Territory)
Tariq ibn Ziyad
Sargon the Great
Governor of al-Andalus
Emperor of Akkad;
Tariq ibn Ziyad was an Arabian general of Berber descent who joined the Arabian army following Musa ibn Nusayr's conquest of the Berbers.
After joining the army, Tariq quickly made a name for himself, claiming victory in the Battle of Guadalete and conquering the Visigothic Kingdom of Hispania (modern-day Spain). The name "Gibraltar" comes from "Jabal Tariq", meaning "Mount of Tariq". But Tariq's story is also a sad one: after becoming governor of Hispania, he was recalled to Damascus, where he would spend the rest of his days in ignominy.
Tariq ibn Ziyad is one of the most important generals in the history of the Iberian Peninsula. He showed great skill in warfare, with history books recording his daring landing at what is now Gibraltar. But following his great victory, he would later experience tragedy to match.
Sargon the Great was born a commoner, and spent his early life as a servant in the Kingdom of Kish. When the people of Umma invaded, Sargon took advantage of the king's weakness to usurp power and founded the Akkadian Empire.
After taking power, Sargon fought many battles to expand his empire's territory. He established the first reserve army force in history, and subsumed all of Sumer, Mari (a city-state on the Euphrates), and the ancient kingdom of Ebla into the Akkadian Empire. After this, he continued westward, expanding into southern and central Anatolia.
Sargon's achievements were not limited to warfare. His empire saw great economic, agricultural, and cultural development, and he centralized the power of the state and appointed governors, allowing the Akkadian Empire to thrive.
Sargon the Great was not merely an accomplished warrior who founded the first great united power between the mighty rivers of the Tigris and the Euphrates. He also focused on economic development, making his empire one of the world's most powerful economies at the time. The Akkadian Empire only stood for a hundred years, but its influence on future generations was undeniable. Sargon was indeed worthy of his title of "King of the Four Corners of the World".'

Margaret I
Starting from the late 1380s, Queen Margaret I reigned over the kingdoms of Denmark, Norway, and Sweden (including Finland at the time), and later united these possessions into the Kalmar Union. She is hailed as one of the most important figures of the Middle Ages and one of Europe's first and greatest queens regnant.
Margaret I was the youngest child of King Valdemar IV of Denmark, and later married King Haakon VI of Norway. After Valdemar's death, Margaret became the de facto regent of Denmark after her 5-year-old son Olaf II succeeded to the Danish throne. After Haakon VI's death, Olaf II inherited the Norwegian throne, making Margaret the de facto ruler of both Denmark and Norway. Her dual regency marked the beginning of a Danish-Norwegian union that would last for more than four centuries.
After the death of Olaf II, Margaret I became Queen of Denmark and Norway suo jure. Supported by the Swedish nobility, Margaret then invaded Sweden and conquered it from the unpopular King Albert. The united Denmark, Norway, and Sweden would become known as the Kalmar Union. To consolidate control, she declared her great-nephew Eric of Pomerania as her heir. With the backing of all three kingdoms' nobility, Eric was crowned as monarch of the Union while Margaret I remained its de facto ruler.
Margaret I died of illness in 1412. Her sarcophagus is located by the altar of the Roskilde Cathedral, and features a carved likeness of the Queen.
BaburI
Babur was the founder of the Mughal Empire. Born Mirza Zahir ud-Din Muhammad in the Fergana Valley, he was the eldest son of Umar Shaikh Mirza, the then ruler of Fergana, and a descendant of Timur, founder of the Timurid Empire.
In 1494, the 12-year-old Babur succeeded to the throne of Fergana, and soon had to defend it from ambitious relatives. Two years later, he conquered Samarkand, but in the process lost control of Fergana. For years, he tried repeatedly to gain control of both territories. Defeated by the Uzbek Khan Muhammad Shaybani in 1501, Babur ended up ruling over neither, and fled south. In 1504 he rallied the remainder of his forces and conquered Kabul. He then managed to regain control of Samarkand for a time, but was forced to abandon it due to both internal and external threats.
Having lost Samarkand again, Babur turned his attention to India. In 1526, he defeated and conquered the Delhi Sultanate at the First Battle of Panipat, marking the beginning of the Mughal Empire. Babur's innovative use of gunpowder was the decisive factor in his victory, and Mughal artillery would be the tool with which they would unite the subcontinent.
An avid writer and reader, Babur penned many poems, songs, and most notably his seminal memoir: the Baburnama. Babur died in 1530 and, in accordance with his wishes, was buried in Kabul.
Heraclius
Heraclius was the first Byzantine Emperor of the Heraclian dynasty. In 608, alongside his father (then the Exarch of Africa), he rebelled against the tyrannical Emperor Phocas. In 610, he defeated Phocas and became the new Emperor.
During Phocas's reign, Byzantium had lost part of its eastern territory to the Sasanian Empire. Heraclius attempted to reconquer this territory, but was soundly defeated. The Sasanians advanced all the way to the Bosphorus, directly threatening the Byzantine capital of Constantinople. Thanks to the city's mighty walls and powerful navy, Heraclius was able to avoid total defeat. Soon after, Heraclius adopted a series of reforms to rebuild and strengthen the Byzantine army. In 627, Heraclius finally defeated the Sasanians at the Battle of Nineveh. The Shahanshah of the Sasanian Empire was overthrown, and his successor soon sued for peace.
The territory Heraclius recaptured soon came under a new threat. The ascendant Rashidun Caliphate from Arabia had managed to swiftly conquer the Sasanian Empire, and made significant inroads into Egypt, Armenia, and Mesopotamia. Faced with this new crisis, Heraclius once again reformed the army, allowing his successors to slow down the Caliphate's expansion.
Under Heraclius's reign, the Byzantine Empire continued to become more Hellenized. Greek became the official language of the Empire, and Heraclius became the first Emperor to officially adopt the Greek title "Basileus".

Dido
Zhuge Liang
The daughter of King Mattan I of Tyre, the Phoenician queen Dido was the mythical founder of Carthage. According to legend, after her brother Pygmalion succeeded to the throne of Tyre, he had Dido's husband murdered to secure his throne and seize her husband's vast wealth. To escape Pygmalion's tyranny, she fled Tyre with her loyal followers, sailing westward across the Mediterranean.
Landing in North Africa, Dido asked the local Berber king Iarbas for a piece of land
only as much land as could be covered by a single oxhide. After the king agreed, Dido cut the oxhide into fine strips, using them to encircle a nearby hill, finding a new home for her and her followers. As more and more Berbers moved in, Dido gradually built her settlement into the wealthy and powerful city-state of Carthage. Located upon the naturally-defensible Hill of Byrsa and in control of the Mediterranean's major arteries, Carthage soon became a military and trade hub. Dido's wise governance only furthered the city's prosperity.
According to some legends, Dido and the Trojan hero Aeneas met and fell in love. Supposedly, after the two were forced to part, the sorrowful queen took her own life after cursing Aeneas and his descendants, though as with most things regarding Dido there is little historical evidence of this. Another, older legend states that as Carthage became more powerful, King Iarbas demanded that Dido marry him, though Dido wished to remain loyal to her late first husband. After pretending to agree to his offer, Dido instead slew herself with her own sword, forever becoming a deified symbol among the Carthaginians for her act of self-sacrifice.
Dido's extraordinary wisdom and leadership have continued to captivate later audiences, and have made her an enduring figure in classical art.;
Chancellor of the state of Shu Han during the Three Kingdoms period, Zhuge Liang is one of the most renowned strategists and administrators in Chinese history. Liu Bei, founding emperor of Shu Han, recruited Zhuge Liang after visiting the latter three times at his thatched cottage, and Zhuge Liang repaid him with his brilliant mind and service.
An excellent advisor, the "Longzhong Plan" he drafted for Liu Bei served as the foundation of Shu Han state policy, advising Liu Bei to ally with Sun Quan of Wu against their common enemy, Cao Cao of Wei. With the aid of Sun Quan's general Lu Su, Zhuge Liang persuaded Sun Quan to join forces against the Wei, leading to one of the period's most famous battles
the Battle of Red Cliffs. As Cao Cao's army headed south to Jingzhou, Zhuge Liang's strategic advice proved invaluable. According to the Romance, Zhuge Liang destroyed Cao Cao's vast armada with flaming ships, then led the combined Shu-Wu force to rout the Wei despite their numerical disadvantage. After Cao Cao's retreat to Xiangyang, the province of Jingzhou became divided between Shu Han, Wu, and Wei.
The Shu-Wu alliance would later break down after Sun Quan formed an alliance with Cao Cao. After a period of internecine conflict, though flanked by the forces of Sun Quan and Cao Cao, Liu Bei declared himself Emperor of Shu Han at Chengdu. After Liu Bei's failed invasion of Wu and later death, Zhuge Liang remained in Chengdu, continuing to advise his son and heir, Liu Shan. He penned the "Chu Shi Biao", a pair of memorials that demonstrated his desire to reclaim the Central Plains and his devotion to Shu Han. He would continue to rectify the government, reform the law, suppress powerful families, pacify rebellions, and invade the north until his death at the age of 53, whereupon he received the posthumous title "The Marquis of Zhongwu".
Zhuge Liang's wisdom, loyalty, and martial prowess have become the stuff of legend. He has become the embodiment of his own maxim, "Spare no effort until your dying day", and the archetypical example of a wise and loyal Confucian official.

Pyrrhusx
Pyrrhus (319/318 - 272 BCE) was the king of the ancient Greek kingdom of Epirus, an eminent military strategist, and one of early Rome's chief rivals for dominion over the Italian peninsula. He was born in the Hellenistic world after the conquests and death of Alexander the Great, a time when Greece was the center of the western world and new city-states were flourishing. Looming over them, however, was the threat of the nascent Roman Republic. Pyrrhus made his small realm a force to be reckoned with through military development and outward expansion.
Pyrrhus led a troubled early life. His father was overthrown when he was a child, but he was able to return home and reclaim his throne with the help of an Illyrian king, though he would later be dethroned once again. With the outbreak of the Fourth War of the Diadochi, the 18-year-old Pyrrhus acquitted himself well at the Battle of Ipsus, but was nonetheless defeated and captured. With the support of Ptolemy I Soter, ruler of Egypt (and his captor), Pyrrhus reconquered Epirus and later a large portion of Macedonia.
Pyrrhus is best known for his war against Rome, battling them for control of southern Italy with an army that even included elephants. At the Battle of Ausculum, Pyrrhus won a very costly victory against the Romans, giving rise to the phrase "Pyrrhic victory" (a victory that costs the victor dearly). Pyrrhus' military acumen was no doubt outstanding, and he was considered one of the finest strategists of his era by his contemporaries. However, his efforts did naught to prevent the eventual fate of ancient Greece, which would over time be invaded and conquered by Rome.=

Pericles~
Pericles (495 - 429 BCE) was an influential politician and strategist in ancient Greece. As ruler and "first citizen" of Athens, he presided over a golden age for the city known as the "Age of Pericles". Born to an aristocratic family in ancient Athens, he first entered Athenian politics in 472 BCE. As leader of the city-state's reformist democratic faction, he outmaneuvered his conservative rival Cimon and rose to the rank of strategos, or military leader. As strategos, he helped restore Athens' preeminence over the Hellenistic world. He was also a renowned orator and beloved by both the citizenry and soldiery of Athens. During his tenure, Pericles fought in the Peloponnesian Wars, when the Athens-led Delian League fought the Sparta-led Peloponnesian League. Pericles died of disease in 429 BCE, early into the second Peloponnesian War, a war that ended the Athenian hegemony over Greece.
Pericles's reign coincided with the rapid development of ancient Greek democracy. His rule saw the flourishing of Greek art and culture, along with the rise of famous philosophers like Socrates and Plato. At a public funeral for the war dead of the Peloponnesian War, Pericles delivered his famous "Funeral Oration", where he extolled the greatness of Athens and lifted the city's spirits. The Funeral Oration has been passed down through the generations as an important part of Athens' cultural heritage.*

Huo Qubing
Huo Qubing was a Chinese general during the reign of Emperor Wu of the Western Han dynasty, as well as the nephew of the famous general Wei Qing and Emperor Wu's wife Empress Wei Zifu. He was known for his tactical agility and talent for long-distance campaigns, winning many battles against the Xiongnu nomads and expanding Han influence into Central Asia.
At the age of 18, Huo Qubing was appointed the "Captain of Piaoyao", leading his troops deep into the desert in expeditions against the Xiongnu. His daring exploits earned him the noble title "Marquis Guanjun". At the age of 19, he was promoted to the rank of cavalry general and dispatched to the Hexi Corridor (in modern-day northwest China) to battle the Xiongnu. Penetrating deep into Xiongnu territory, he nearly captured the son of the Chanyu (ruler of the Xiongnu) alive. The Hexi campaign largely halted the once-devastating Xiongnu raids on the northwest Han frontier, which helped reduce the empire's reliance on corv
e labor. At 21, Huo Qubing led 50,000 cavalry across the Gobi Desert in pursuit of the Chanyu. Upon arriving at the Khentii Mountains, Huo Qubing famously performed a series of religious rituals, symbolizing the Han defeat of the Xiongnu. He continued to pursue the Xiongnu, driving them back to the northern Gobi (Mobei).
At 23 or 24 years old, Huo Qubing, having spent almost his entire adult life in combat, suddenly died of illness. Emperor Wu was greatly saddened by his death, awarding him the posthumous title of Marquis Jinghuan. The emperor ordered the construction of a tomb for him in the shape of the Qilian Mountains he traversed in his campaigns. Years after his death, Huo Qubing's military prowess and achievements continued to be commemorated and praised in countless literary works.@

Justinian I
Justinian I reigned over the Byzantine Empire from 527 to 565 CE. Known as the "emperor who never sleeps" for his diligence, under Justinian's rule the Byzantine Empire experienced a period of relative stability and prosperity.
Justinian I succeeded to the Byzantine throne after the death of his uncle Justin I. Upon taking the throne he took many political and military measures to consolidate his rule and restore the borders of the Roman Empire. Under his rule, the Byzantine army defeated the Sasanians in several battles, ending decades of hostilities with a short-lived (and ironically-named) peace treaty called the "Perpetual Peace". In the Gothic Wars, the Byzantines defeated the Ostrogoths in Italy and secured the empire's position in Western Europe. One of Justinian's generals and the commander of these wars of re-conquest was the famed Belisarius.
In addition to his military accomplishments, Justinian I introduced an important legal framework called the Corpus Juris Civilis, better known by the name of its first section: the Code of Justinian. A combination of old Roman laws and new Byzantine laws, it became one of the most important legal codices in European history and had a profound influence on later European legal systems.
Another of his many accomplishments was the reconstruction of the Hagia Sophia. He also led the empire through the Plague of Justinian, the first recorded outbreak of the first plague pandemic and an ailment that Justinian himself is said to have contracted and recovered from. As evidenced by his moniker "Justinian the Great", he is generally considered to be one of the greatest emperors in Byzantine history.3

Gorgo
Laconian Eagle
Gorgo reigned as Queen of Sparta in the early 5th century BCE. She ruled Sparta alongside her husband Leonidas I, and played an important role in the war against the invading Persian Empire.
Under the reign of the Achaemenids, Persia had grown into a gargantuan empire, and sought to expand its reach into the central Mediterranean. Led by Leonidas I and Gorgo, the Spartans stalled the Persians at Thermopylae, buying time for the Athenian navy and playing a key role in Greece's eventual victory.
Much has been written about Gorgo in historical records, at a time when even important women were often confined to footnotes and margins. This speaks volumes about her significant influence over Spartan politics and her prestige in the eyes of her contemporaries. It is said that, at just eight years old, she was already providing advice to her father, then the king of Sparta. After she became queen, it was she who first discovered the Persians' impending invasion.w
After this troop is defeated, all nearby governor troops will deal {s1}% more damage to event enemies for {p2} seconds.

Smite damage is affected by bonuses to normal damage instead of bonuses to skill damage.I
Emperor Wu of Han, born Liu Che, ascended the Dragon Throne at the age of 16. The seventh emperor of the Western Han dynasty, Liu Che ruled over China for 53 years, the third-longest reign in Chinese history.
Liu Che inherited an extremely wealthy empire, and leveraged this wealth to greatly expand the Han dynasty's borders. His many conquests are reflected in his posthumous name "Emperor Wu", "wu" meaning "martial" or "warlike" in Chinese. However, years of warfare inevitably placed a heavy burden on the empire, and in his later years he focused more on internal reforms. These reforms laid the groundwork for the golden age that would follow.
One of Liu Che's edicts enforced "partition" succession for the great princes of the empire. Upon their deaths, their holdings would have to be distributed amongst their children, gradually reducing the size of their fiefdoms and their influence over the empire. He also replaced the old system of hereditary bureaucrats with an early form of the imperial examination system, which would have massive implications for the rest of Chinese history.

King of Assyria
Wolf of Teutoburgu
Ashurbanipal ruled the Neo-Assyrian Empire from 669 to 627 BCE. Under his rule, Assyria reached the height of its territorial extent and military might.
Ashurbanipal succeeded his father Esarhaddon to the throne. Though he had inherited a mighty empire, he was not content to rest on his laurels. During his reign, he fought countless wars of expansion, extending Assyria's already-vast territory into Egypt and Anatolia. 
For all of Ashurbanipal's military accomplishments, Assyria was in its twilight years, and beset by countless rebellions. He turned his attention inwards, suppressing internal dissent with an iron fist and rivers of blood. Ultimately, this did little to reverse Assyria's decline, which would only hasten after Ashurbanipal's death.
Empires may die, but civilizations live on. Over the course of his long reign, he devoted considerable resources to constructing the Library of Ashurbanipal, where he amassed a collection of texts so vast that it would not be surpassed until the Library of Alexandria, some 400 years later. 
The Library was eventually destroyed in a fire, but since all of its texts were inscribed on clay tablets, this actually helped preserve them by hardening the clay. This mountain of invaluable knowledge lay undisturbed until the 19th century, when it was discovered by archaeologists. Among its many treasures was the now-famous Epic of Gilgamesh.~
Arminius (18 BCE - 21 CE), known in German as Hermann, was chieftain of the Cherusci tribe, commander of an alliance of Germanic tribes, and a German national hero. His German name means "Man of War", which would later prove apt.
The son of a Germanic chieftain allied to Rome, Hermann was sent to Rome as a hostage, where he was educated. Though raised in Rome, he had never forgotten his roots, nor the weight of Rome's iron fist upon his people. After being dispatched to Germania as a Roman auxiliary commander, he began to secretly reach out to other Germanic tribes, hoping to unite them against the Romans.
His efforts culminated in the Battle of the Teutoburg Forest. The Germanic tribes were intimately familiar with forest combat, while the narrow and muddy roads made it difficult for the Romans to form their vaunted battle lines. Catching the Romans in an ambush, Hermann and his army wiped out three entire legions and took their treasured eagle banners, an event that would be immortalized in Germany's national mythology.
Though Hermann's victory secured the freedom of his people east of the Rhine, it would be far from the last battle he would fight. The Romans remained encamped across the river, and were not the kind to take such a humiliation lightly. Yet the liberator of Germania had made a god bleed, and would do so again if it secured his people a future free of Roman tyranny.

The Shorestone Shield
Lapulapu was the datu (chief) of Mactan Island, located in the Visayas archipelago in what is now the Philippines. When Ferdinand Magellan arrived in the region in 1521, Lapulapu refused to pay homage or tribute to the Spanish Empire or its local allies. On April 27, 1521, in response to this act of defiance, Magellan attacked Mactan with a force of 49 soldiers. Lapulapu fought back fiercely, defeating Magellan (who was slain in the battle) and driving the Spanish from the island.
Lapulapu then left Mactan with his family and friends, thereafter disappearing from the history records. One local legend states that he actually turned into a stone on the shores of Mactan, and has been silently guarding the island ever since.
Gonzalo de C
rdoba
Father of the Tercioj
Gonzalo Fern
ndez de C
rdoba (1453-1515), known by his nickname "El Gran Capit
n", was a Spanish general and politician, as well as an enormously influential innovator in the field of warfare. He was one of the first European commanders to truly integrate firearms in his tactics, and his innovations can be said to mark the beginning of "modern warfare". His highly-organized pike-and-shot formations made Spain Europe's preeminent military power for over a century. Beyond his military achievements, he was also appointed Viceroy of Naples (at the time under Spanish rule) and inducted into the Order of Santiago.
Gajah Mada
Jirnnodharay
Gajah Mada (c. 1290-1364) was the mahapatih (prime minister) of the Majapahit Empire, based in what is now Indonesia. Serving in that office for three decades and under three different monarchs, he is credited with leading Majapahit to its greatest glory. He is known for swearing an oath known as the "Sumpah Palapa", vowing to not "enjoy pleasure" until he had unified all of Nusantara (Maritime Southeast Asia). He nearly succeeded in that endeavor, conquering lands in modern-day Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, Brunei, the Philippines, and East Timor, and has since become a celebrated symbol of Indonesian unity and nationhood.

Greetings, governor. "Scaling Mode" has been enabled for this event. In Scaling Mode, the difficulty of all Ceroli chieftains will be adjusted to match your power.
If your kingdom hasn't reached Season 2 yet, you will only be able to select normal difficulty for this event, and the rewards you can earn will be based on your power.
On normal difficulty, you can choose to be matched with governors of different power levels from you. In addition, all members of your team will receive extra combat buffs and rewards.
In Season 2 and later, "Legend Mode" will be enabled for this event. You will need to meet certain power requirements to unlock it.
In Legend Mode, the Ceroli chieftains will be far more powerful, but the rewards you earn will be far greater in turn. Be sure that you're fully prepared before you undertake this challenge.;

Eleanor of Aquitaine
Belisarius
Eleanor of Aquitaine (1122 - 1204) was one of the most powerful and influential women in European history. The consort of two kings and the mother of two more, many kings came and went over the course of her long life, but the power and influence of the Duchess of Aquitaine never wavered.
Eleanor inherited the Duchy of Aquitaine at age 14, immediately putting her in control of nearly one third of the Kingdom of France. The crown was covetous of and threatened by her wealth, and she was quickly married off to King Louis VII of France. Eleanor found life at court (and her husband) tedious. Having failed to produce a male heir after 15 years, the marriage between Louis and Eleanor was annulled.
Not long after the annulment, Eleanor married Henry Plantagenet, Count of Anjou. Together, the pair controlled most of France. When Henry became King of England, Eleanor was crowned as his Queen. Their marriage would produce three daughters and five sons, one of whom was the famed Richard the Lionheart.
From childhood, Eleanor took an interest in many pursuits not common for women of her era, from literature and art to riding and hunting. She broke the mold for what it meant to be a woman, wife, and ruler in medieval Europe, even leading her own knights in the Second Crusade.
Eleanor was imprisoned in England for over a decade for supporting her son's rebellion against her husband, though she was freed after Richard took the throne. She spent her later years arranging marriages for her children and quelling rebellions. She once crossed the Pyrenees, at nearly 80 years old, to secure a marriage for her granddaughter. Thanks to her tireless efforts, the majority of her children ended up in happy marriages, spreading the bloodline and earning her the nickname "the Grandmother of Europe".
Whether as duchess, queen, or dowager, Eleanor of Aquitaine was a lily unlike any other; one that flourished in open fields rather than behind palace walls.
Belisarius was a Byzantine general in the service of Emperor Justinian I. Entering Justinian's service as the commander of his personal guard, he went on to win countless important victories for the Eastern Roman Empire.
Belisarius's quelling of the Nika Riots of 532 helped consolidate Justinian's rule, and earned him the emperor's trust. He led successive campaigns against the Vandals and Ostrogoths, retaking much of Rome's former western territory. Belisarius became a symbol of Rome's enduring legacy, and his elite Bucellarii regiment became the stuff of legends.
With his Bucellarii, Belisarius won impossible victory after impossible victory. His downfall would come not on the battlefield, but in the imperial court. Baseless accusations whispered by his rivals would poison his reputation with Justinian, and from then on Belisarius would never command another army.
Belisarius's role in the restoration of Roman lands and his embodiment of the Roman spirit have earned him the nickname "Last of the Romans".-

Whenever the Shamans use a skill, the Divine Drum will gain Energy (up to {p1} Energy). If the Drum is passed to another Shaman, its Energy will reset to 0. If the Drum reaches max Energy, it will deal direct damage to all enemy troops on the battlefield (Damage Factor {p2}).
William Wallace
Scipio Aemilianus
Guardian of Scotland
Conqueror of Carthage
William Wallace was a Scottish knight and nobleman who served as the leader of the Scottish forces in the early parts of the First Scottish War of Independence.
In 1296, the Kingdom of Scotland was invaded and occupied by King Edward I of England. Shortly thereafter, along with other like-minded rebels, Wallace launched a campaign of resistance. More and more Scottish nobles flocked to his cause, culminating in September 1297 at the Battle of Stirling Bridge, where he won a major victory against the English.
He was appointed sole Guardian of Scotland (essentially a regent), and remained as such until 1298, when he was defeated at the Battle of Falkirk. He was captured by the English in 1305 and taken to London, where he was convicted of treason. For his defiance, Edward I ordered Wallace to be gruesomely tortured and beheaded.
The Scottish struggle for freedom did not end with William Wallace's death, and through steel and blood they would go on to win their independence from England some twenty years later. For his heroic spirit and being one of the first to rise against the English, Wallace gained worldwide fame and became an icon of Scottish independence.W
Scipio Aemilianus (185-129 BCE, full name Publius Cornelius Scipio Africanus Aemilianus) was a prominent commander and statesman of the Roman Republic. Known for both his military prowess and his political acumen, he served two terms as Consul of Rome.
Scipio Aemilianus's most famous achievement was his siege and capture of the city of Carthage, which marked the decisive end to the century-long struggle for supremacy between Rome and Carthage. Besides his campaigns in North Africa, Aemilianus is also known for his conquests in Hispania, where he showcased his strategic shrewdness in capturing the Celtiberian city of Numantia. He was a gifted orator and a patron of scholars, and was always accompanied by an entourage of writers and luminaries discussing art, philosophy, and science.
With his strategic, political, and intellectual brilliance, Scipio Aemilianus accomplished many legendary feats throughout his life and during his time as Consul. He cemented Rome's long-running conquest of the western Mediterranean, and his legacy can be felt today in the influence of Roman culture in those lands.

Shajar al-Durr
Choe Yeong
Tree of Pearls
Lord Mumin
Over the course of her remarkable life, Shajar al-Durr rose from a Mamluk slave to one of the few female rulers in Egyptian history.
Shajar al-Durr began her rise to power as a slave of As-Salih Ayyub, who would later go on to marry her and become Sultan of Egypt. In 1249, her husband died while defending Egypt from the Seventh Crusade. To preserve stability and unity in the face of the invading Crusaders, Shajar al-Durr cleverly concealed her husband's death, issuing orders and commanding the army "in the name of the Sultan". With the help of her Mamluk allies, she defeated the Crusaders and ascended to the throne of Egypt, becoming Sultan in her own right.
Besides defeating the Seventh Crusade, Shajar al-Durr's reign laid the foundation for Mamluk rule in Egypt, which would last for hundreds of years.
The political and religious environment of the time, however, was not conducive to a female monarch, and her reign as Sultan only lasted for three months (though she married the new Sultan and remained an influential political figure). Nevertheless, her incredible journey from slave to Sultan, political acumen, and military talents have made her an icon of Egyptian history and a symbol of female empowerment.%
Choe Yeong (also Romanized as "Ch'oe Y
ng" and "Choi Young") was a prominent general from the final years of Korea's Goryeo dynasty.
From an early age, Choe Yeong demonstrated considerable military acumen, winning several major battles both at home and abroad. In 1374, he put down the Mokho Rebellion, an uprising of Mongol horse herders on Tamna Island (known now as Jeju Island). In 1376, Japanese pirates ("waegu") attacked and captured the city of Gongju. Together with his then-subordinate Yi Seong-Gye, Choe Yeong defeated the pirates and retook the city. 
In 1388, Choe Yeong persuaded King U of Goryeo to invade the Liaodong Peninsula and seize it from the nascent Ming dynasty. Yi Seong-Gye was dispatched to lead this invasion. Upon reaching Wihwado, an island in the Amnok River, Yi Seong-Gye decided to turn his army around and launch a coup against the Goryeo dynasty. Choe Yeong was arrested, exiled, and ultimately executed. The "Wihwado Retreat", as this incident later became known, marked the end of the Goryeo dynasty and the establishment of the Joseon dynasty.
Though Choe Yeong remains a somewhat controversial figure, his patriotic spirit and military acumen were widely admired by later generations, and he is still considered an important political and military leader in Korean history.,

Ragnar Lodbrokf
Ragnar Lodbrok (also spelled "Lothbrok" or "lo
k") was a 9th-century Viking warrior and leader. He lived a life of adventure, reaving a path of destruction through much of Europe and parts of North Africa. Though it is unclear how much of his life was historical fact, we do know that he was one of the most important heroes in the Northern European canon.
He is best known for his conquests of England, leading his Viking fleet in several devastating raids of Anglo-Saxon coastal settlements. In 845, he captured and plundered the city of Paris, forcing King Charles the Bald of West Francia to pay a large ransom in exchange for peace. Ragnar's boldness, strategic vision, and ambition made him a symbol of the Viking Age and a paragon for the Vikings that came after. His expeditions brought great wealth to his homeland, greatly enhanced the prestige of the Vikings, and paved the way for future, even greater conquests.
Even Ragnar's end was fittingly dramatic. One legend has it that, while leading a daring (or foolhardy) invasion of Northumbria with just two ships, Ragnar was captured and executed by King 
lla of Northumbria. Even after he died, the legend of Ragnar would be carried on by his many famous sons, like Bj
rn Ironside, Ivar the Boneless, Halfdan Ragnarsson, and Sigurd Snake-in-the-Eye. In 865, Ragnar's sons led a massive invasion of England known as the "Great Heathen Army", where they extracted gruesome vengeance against King 
lla and carved a massive realm out of Northern and Eastern England.
For his daring and wisdom, the story of Ragnar Lodbrok still captivates modern audiences around the world.

Philip IIW
Philip II was king of ancient Macedon, reigning from 359 BCE to 336 BCE. Though he is sometimes overshadowed by the accomplishments of his son, Alexander the Great, Philip of Macedon was an accomplished conqueror and statesman in his own right.
Through military reform, diplomacy, and sometimes brute force, Philip II transformed Macedon from the backwater of the ancient Hellenic world into its hegemon. On the military end, he is credited with developing the famed Macedonian phalanxes, equipping them with an ultra-long pike known as the "sarissa". On the diplomatic front, he expanded Macedonian influence through marriages, alliances, and diplomatic maneuvering.
Philip's most famous battle was the 338 BCE Battle of Chaeronea, where he defeated the combined forces of Athens and Thebes, cementing Macedon as the dominant power in Greece. Shortly after his victory, Philip formed the League of Corinth, a federation of Greek city-states under his leadership.
In 336 BCE, Philip II began a campaign against Persia. Before it could come to anything, however, he was assassinated at his daughter's wedding.
Stephen III
Stephen III (1433-1504) was Voievod of Moldavia and one of its greatest rulers. Though surrounded on all sides by powerful and expansionist empires, he was able to defend his nation's independence. In 1457, he overthrew his uncle Peter III Aaron, beginning his 47-year reign.
As ruler of Moldavia, he was forced to contend with three extremely powerful neighbors: the Ottoman Empire, the Kingdom of Hungary, and the Kingdom of Poland. He was able to defend Moldavian independence by playing these powers against one another, and also by sheer force of arms. His most celebrated victory came at the 1475 Battle of Vaslui, where he inflicted a decisive defeat on a much larger Ottoman army.
Besides his military accomplishments, Stephen III was a devout adherent of the Romanian Orthodox Church, building many churches and monasteries across Moldavia that greatly contributed to its cultural, artistic, and literary heritage. It is little wonder that he is known as 
tefan cel Mare ("the Great").
John Hunyadi
John Hunyadi was a prominent Hungarian commander and politician, best known for defending Hungary from an Ottoman invasion.
Perhaps Hunyadi's most famous accomplishment was his defense of Belgrade. In 1453, the Ottoman Sultan Mehmed II completed his conquest of Constantinople, and soon after turned his sights onto the Balkans. In 1456, he brought his cannons to Belgrade, then a part of the Kingdom of Hungary, and laid siege to the city. Leading a motley crew of Hungarian soldiers and various other volunteers, Hunyadi forced the Ottomans to retreat.
The victory at Belgrade was widely celebrated, and is believed to have temporarily halted the rapid expansion of the Ottoman Empire. During the siege, Pope Pius II ordered churches across Europe to ring their bells every day at noon, calling on Christendom to pray for the defenders of Belgrade.
John Hunyadi died of an illness in 1456, less than a month after the end of the siege. He continues to be hailed as one of Hungary's greatest heroes to this day.R

Siyaj K'ak'
Wak Chanil Ajaw
Fire is Born
Lady Six Sky
Siyaj K'ak' (Mayan for "Fire is Born") is generally (but not universally) believed to have come from the city-state of Teotihuacan. Located in Central Mexico, near modern-day Mexico City, Teotihuacan was one of the most powerful states in Mesoamerica at the time. Much like Teotihuacan itself, however, the specifics of his background are difficult to confirm with the sources we have available. Based on his later achievements, he likely received some sort of training, becoming a proficient tactician and diplomat.
Serving in the army of a Teotihuacano king known only as "Spearthrower Owl" (a literal description of his name glyph, which consists of an owl holding an atlatl), he rose quickly through the ranks. He seems to have earned the king's trust, as he was entrusted with the task of expanding Teotihuacan's influence southwards. In 378 CE, he led an army to the lowland Maya city of Tikal (located in modern-day Guatemala). 
The king of Tikal died the day Siyaj K'ak' arrived, after which he installed Yax Nuun Ahiin, a son of Spearthrower Owl, as king. Serving as regent of Tikal and an important regional warlord, Siyaj K'ak' reshaped the political landscape of the area and brought in Teotihuacan culture.
Siyaj K'ak's rule over Tikal marks the start of rapidly-expanding Central Mexican influence on lowland Maya art and architecture, which can still be observed in many surviving Maya ruins. Though he never formally ruled Tikal, he remained an extremely influential figure in the region until his death some time in the fifth century.
Wak Chanil Ajaw, whose name literally translates into "Lady Six Sky", was an influential ruler of the Maya city-state Naranjo ("Wak Kab'nal" in Mayan) in the 7th century CE. She was born in the city-state of Dos Pilas (its Mayan name is unknown, but it used the emblem glyph "Mutal", the same as Tikal), a daughter of its king. Though originally an outpost of Tikal, Dos Pilas had secured its independence from Tikal and sought to build a coalition against it. Wak Chanil Ajaw was sent to Naranjo as part of a political marriage to secure its support.
The throne of Naranjo lay empty at the time and the city was in decline, and thus Wak Chanil Ajaw was given the additional task of establishing a new royal bloodline there and restoring the city's prominence. Though her sons would hold the actual title of "king", Wak Chanil Ajaw governed the city as regent. Thanks to her political, military, and administrative acumen, Naranjo re-emerged as a regional power.
Known most for her military achievements, Wak Chanil Ajaw launched a series of wars and raids that expanded the borders and influence of Naranjo. She also seems to have contributed to Maya timekeeping, an important contribution for a culture that placed immense, virtually theological importance in time and dates. Wak Chanil Ajaw is now remembered as one of the most remarkable and powerful female rulers in Maya history, who brought glory and prosperity to Naranjo..

Catherine de' Medici
Narses
Shadow Queen
Grand Chamberlain
Catherine de' Medici (1519 - 1589) was a member of the Medici family, a powerful banking family and hereditary rulers of Florence. Through her marriage to Henry II of France, she became Queen Consort of France. After the death of Henry II, she wielded enormous influence over French politics as the mother of three Kings of France: Francis II, Charles IX, and Henry III.
Presiding over the turbulent years of the French Wars of Religion and a scheming, squabbling court, Catherine worked to ensure the continued survival of the royal House of Valois through diplomacy and political maneuvering. Though a Catholic, Catherine attempted to maintain a balance between Catholics and the Protestant Huguenots. She supported several attempts at religious tolerance, including the Edicts of Amboise and Saint-Germain, though these efforts ultimately failed to de-escalate the conflict. The 1572 St. Bartholomew's Day Massacre of Huguenots only served to deepen the kingdom's religious divisions.
Catherine was not only influential in the political realm, she also contributed immensely to the development of the French Renaissance. A renowned patron of artists, writers, and especially architects, her support greatly increased the cultural prestige of the House of Valois and France as a whole.I
Narses (ca. 478-573) was a prominent general and statesman in the Eastern Roman Empire during the reign of Justinian I. An Armenian eunuch of somewhat uncertain origins, Narses held several important civilian and military posts throughout his career, playing a key role in the Eastern Roman Empire's expansion and defense.
Narses was a colleague of Belisarius, and the two often fought alongside each other in their service to Justinian, though they did not get along. After Belisarius was relieved of his command, it was Narses who would complete the Roman reconquest of Italy. He would go on to govern Italy for over a decade, quickly and effectively crushing Gothic resistance and other Germanic invaders. He would eventually be recalled to Constantinople, however, due to his persistent conflicts with the local Italian nobility.
Though not as famous as his contemporary Belisarius, Narses's military accomplishments and contributions to the Empire were considerable. He would be remembered and widely celebrated by later generations as one of the finest commanders of the Late Roman period.

Subutai
Sword of the Khan
Subutai (c. 1176-1248) was a prominent commander and strategist of the Mongol Empire. One of the "Four Dogs of Temujin", Genghis Khan's four most trusted generals, he was renowned as a brilliant strategist and commander who developed innovative tactics centuries ahead of their time.
An early follower of Genghis Khan, Subutai served as an important commander in the Mongol conquests of Central Asia, China, and Russia. He excelled at agile tactics, especially in long, far-reaching expeditions, often relying on lightning-fast raids to mislead, divide, and outflank his enemies. These precise strikes gave the Mongols the element of surprise, and they played a crucial role in the expansion of the empire.
Not only was Subutai a brave warrior, he was also known for his diligence, determination, and loyalty. In an empire that would later be characterized by ambition and infighting, Subutai stands out for having never sought personal power at the expense of his khan. His life represented the pinnacle of Mongol military glory, and despite being overlooked by western scholars for many years, he is now regarded as one of the greatest commanders in history.

Arthur Pendragon
Once and Future King
Though the legendary Brythonic king known as "King Arthur" probably originates from early Welsh mythology, the best known form of his legend comes from the corpus of medieval chivalric literature known collectively as "the Matter of Britain". 
In this literary tradition, King Arthur was the illegitimate son of Uther Pendragon, King of the Britons, and the famous beauty Igraine. He is protected and secretly raised by Merlin (portrayed in some sources as a wizard), remaining unaware of his own origins. When Uther dies without an apparent heir, Arthur inadvertently proves himself to be the rightful King of the Britons by pulling out the famous "sword in the stone" (in traditional accounts, the sword is actually in an anvil on top of the stone). 
As king, Arthur founds the Knights of the Round Table, a chivalric order where all members are equal, dedicated to pursuing the Holy Grail and maintaining peace in Arthur's kingdom. Another well-known aspect of the King Arthur tale is his legendary magical sword Excalibur, which is granted to him by the mystical Lady of the Lake after the sword he pulled from the stone breaks.
In the traditional tale, King Arthur meets his end at the Battle of Camlann (later renamed to the "Battle of Salisbury") when he is stabbed by Mordred, his own illegitimate son. In his final act of glory, King Arthur uses the last of his strength to kill Mordred in turn. Before he dies, King Arthur orders Excalibur returned to the Lady of the Lake.
Though King Arthur likely never existed (the medieval depiction of him certainly did not), he served as the model for chivalry for medieval knights and princes, and he remains an extremely important cultural hero for the peoples of Britain.5

COMMANDER STRATEGIES

 <b>Overview</b>
Borrow the strength of a new Legendary commander!
 <b>How to Participate</b>
1. The event will last for <color=#379f1f>{p1} days</color>. During the event, you'll be able to try out the new Legendary commander.
2. You can use this trial commander in Ark of Osiris, Champions of Olympia, Sunset Canyon, Lost Canyon, Ceroli Crisis, and other Campaign game modes. <color=#379f1f>You will not be able to use them for kingdom/Lost Kingdom map battles or to garrison your Wall</color>.
3. At the end of the event, the trial commander will be automatically removed from your roster.
 <b>Important Notes</b>
1. The trial commander will be level 60 and 6 stars. If you already personally own the trial commander and choose to take part in the trial, you will not be able to use sculptures to upgrade their skills during the trial period.
2. You can equip trial commanders with <color=#379f1f>equipment</color> and <color=#379f1f>armaments</color>. They will be automatically unequipped when the event ends.
3. This event is only available to kingdoms that have reached the <color=#379f1f>Season 3 Eve of the Crusade</color>. If you immigrate to a kingdom in Season 2 or earlier, the trial commander will be automatically recalled.
This commander will be recalled at the end of the trial period.
Trial commanders do not count as having been "recruited" (e.g. if an event only allows you to select commanders you've already recruited). Legendary Commander Sculptures cannot be used to upgrade a trial commander's skills.
In Balanced Mode, you will only be able to use commanders available in your current season. Commander training (levels, skill levels, etc.) will be equalized, but you can adjust their talents, armaments, and equipment.

 Daigongen
Tokugawa Ieyasu (1543-1616) was a preeminent military leader and statesman during Japan's turbulent Sengoku period. With his strategic vision and steadfast character, he unified the fractious warring states of Japan under a single banner. As founder of the Tokugawa Shogunate and the last of Japan's Three Great Unifiers, he is remembered today as one of the single most important figures in Japanese history.
In 1603, after defeating his greatest rivals at Sekigahara, Tokugawa Ieyasu officially received the title of sh
gun, marking the beginning of the Edo period and the 265-year rule of the Tokugawa clan. The relative political stability he ushered in resulted in one of the most remarkable periods of cultural, economic, and intellectual development in Japanese history. During his reign, Ieyasu focused on domestic unification and integration. His groundbreaking reforms included strengthening central authority, creating new regulatory bodies, and bringing Japan's countless feudal lords to heel.
After Tokugawa Ieyasu's death, he was given the posthumous title "T
 Daigongen", which translates into "Great Gongen (a syncretic Buddhist-Shinto deity), Light of the East". Several hundred Shinto shrines dedicated to him, known as "T
-gu", were built over the course of the Edo period, with the most famous being the Nikk
 in Toshigi Prefecture. Though his historical reputation took a hit after the Meiji Restoration, he remains one of the most revered figures in Japanese history.
Bai Qi
Deity of Death
Bai Qi (ca. 332-257 BCE) was a renowned general and strategist during China's Warring States period, and one of the most important figures in the rise of the Qin state. He was a man known for both his supreme military acumen and his utter ruthlessness, for he was willing to do whatever it took to bring the Qin to greatness. His conquests turned the Qin into the unquestioned hegemon of China, laying the groundwork for its future unification of the Seven Warring States.
Bai Qi led numerous far-reaching campaigns throughout his life, becoming known for his decisiveness, adaptability, and brutality towards his enemies. His most famous victories were at the battles of Yique, Changping, and Yanying, which greatly weakened the Qin's rivals and made it by far the most powerful of the Seven States. He emphasized logistics and training, remaining strict in his tactics but flexible in his overall strategy. Virtually undefeated on the battlefield, he is one of the select few commanders in Chinese history to have received the title "Ever-Victorious General". 
Known as one of the Four Great Generals of the Warring States for his undeniable achievements and strategic skill, Bai Qi would have a profound influence on future Chinese military theory.

Shapur Ix
Besides being a valiant warrior king, Shapur I was also a brilliant administrator who placed great emphasis on the economic, religious, and cultural life of his empire. He built many remarkable structures across the empire, including the city of Bishapur. While largely tolerant of the many faiths in his empire, Shapur I also sponsored a revival of Zoroastrianism, which would become the state religion of the empire, and the founding of Manichaeism, which would spread rapidly over the next few centuries. His reign formed the foundation for the Sasanian Empire's future prosperity and made it one of the world's foremost powers.
Shahanshah
Qin Shi Huang
The future Qin Shi Huang was born Ying Zheng (259-210 BCE), a prince of Qin. At the time of his birth, China was divided into seven warring kingdoms. Over the past century, a series of Legalist reforms, westward expansions, and military victories had transformed Qin from a rural backwater into the most powerful of the seven states.
In 246 BCE, at just 13 years old, Ying Zheng became King of Qin. True power lay in the hands of Chancellor L
 Buwei and the other regents, but by 235 Ying Zheng had managed to seize full control of the government. From 230 to 221, through cunning ploys and brilliant generalship, King Zheng successively conquered the states of Han, Zhao, Wei, Chu, Yan, and Qi. With the seven states finally united, Ying Zheng declared himself China's first emperor (huangdi), changing his name to Qin Shi Huangdi (First Emperor of Qin).
With the seven states unified, Qin Shi Huang went to work consolidating them into a single nation. Over the 500 years since the decline of the Zhou kings, China's already-fragmented institutions had drifted even further apart. With an iron fist, the new emperor centralized power, created a new administrative structure, and unified China's writing system, measurements, and laws. He connected the country with new canals and fortified it with new defensive works, the most notable of which was the first segment of the Great Wall. Though his dynasty would not long outlive him, Qin Shi Huang's unification of the states and his conception of a single China formed the basis of legitimacy for future Chinese dynasties.
Much of his later reign was dedicated to his pursuit of immortality, contracting scholars, magicians, and alchemists across China to find a solution. As evidenced by his death in 211 BCE, they failed. Even in death, the First Emperor was not one to be outdone. His massive mausoleum and the thousands of "Terracotta Warriors" contained within laid the foundation for the image of the Chinese emperor as a divine sovereign.

Archimedes
Father of Mathematics`
Archimedes (c. 287 - 212 BCE) was a groundbreaking mathematician, scientist, and inventor, referred to by some as "the father of mathematics". Born on the island of Sicily in the ancient Greek city-state of Syracuse, Archimedes devoted his entire life to science. His works have had a foundational impact on the fields of geometry, calculus, fluid mechanics, statics, and more.
Among his many achievements, one of his most notable includes formulating the law of buoyancy (the "Archimedes' principle"). He was also a prolific inventor, inventing the "Archimedes' screw" (a type of screw pump still in use today) and strange war machines used to defend Syracuse from the Romans (though many of these, like his "iron hand", were so fantastical that many historians question if they were real or not). He is often quoted as saying, "Give me a lever and fulcrum, and I shall move the world."
In the area of mathematics, he was a pioneer in the study of infinitesimals (a predecessor to calculus), was possibly the first person to find the area and volume of a sphere, and proposed a new method for approximating the value of pi. His scientific curiosity and innovative thinking, reflected in his countless tangible contributions, have profoundly influenced future scholars.
Though the Roman commander wanted him spared out of respect (and because of his potential usefulness), Archimedes was killed by an unknowing Roman soldier when they captured Syracuse. His apocryphal final words were "Do not disturb my circles" (referring to mathematical figures he was drawing in the dust), a testament to how he devoted his life to learning.
Mary I
Bloody Mary
Mary I Tudor, Queen of England and Ireland, was the only surviving child of Henry VIII and his first wife, Catherine of Aragon. She was the first woman to officially rule England in her own right, beginning a long legacy of influential British queens. 
A bright child, she was fluent in multiple languages and was a talented musician, but the breakdown of her parents' marriage and her devout Catholicism caused her to be excluded from the succession. After the death of Mary's younger brother Edward VI in 1553, the throne instead went to her distant relative Lady Jane Grey, the "Nine Days Queen". With the support of most of the nobility and the commons, Mary deposed the Lady Grey after only nine days.
In 1554, she married Philip the Prudent, prince (and future king) of Spain. The marriage was deeply unpopular with the increasingly Protestant population, sparking insurrections across the country. Her reign also saw the loss of Calais
England's last continental holding
and a declining economy (though this was mostly the fault of her spendthrift father). Mary died in 1558, at the age of 42. With no children to succeed her, the throne went to her half-sister, Elizabeth I.
Mary I's driving agenda throughout her reign was the restoration of Catholicism in England. To that end, she worked to reverse her father's religious reforms and restore relations with the Catholic world. Her often-violent repression of Protestants earned her the nickname "Bloody Mary" by later historians, though modern historians continue to debate how much her brutal reputation was justified.
Though still controversial, Mary's short reign proved memorable. Though they had yet to bear fruit during her own reign, many of her efforts would prove beneficial to the reign of her sister, a golden age of English history.

Matthias I
Corvinusx
Matthias I (1443-1490), also known as "Matthias Corvinus", was one of Hungary's greatest kings. In addition to reigning as King of Hungary and Croatia from 1458 to 1490, he also claimed the thrones of the Kingdom of Bohemia and the Archduchy of Austria.
Son of the national hero John Hunyadi, Matthias Corvinus was renowned for his political acumen, even-handedness, and martial talent. His political, judicial, and economic reforms centralized power in the kingdom and made the crown tremendously wealthy. Developing on the ideas of his father, Matthias used this wealth to build his famous Black Army, one of Europe's first standing armies. This extremely potent force turned Hungary into one of the greatest powers in Europe.
He was perhaps better known, however, for both his patronage of learning and his own intellectual curiosity. Unlike other monarchs at the time, the well-read Matthias was eager to embrace the Renaissance ideas fomenting in Italy, sponsoring art, music, and academia. His personal library, the Bibliotheca Corviniana, became one of the most renowned libraries in all of Europe, attracting many scholars and artists to his court.
His reign is regarded as the peak of the Hungarian golden age, and he remains a national hero to this day. The wise king "Matthias the Just" became the subject of folk tales, with the common folk mourning his death as "the loss of justice".
Afonso de Albuquerque
Caesar of the East
Afonso de Albuquerque (1453-1515) was a visionary commander, admiral, and administrator in service to the Portuguese Empire. Born into a noble family with strong historic ties to the Portuguese crown, he devoted himself to the maritime expansion of the empire and establishing its near total control over the Indian Ocean.
After capturing several key port cities along the Indian Ocean trade route, Afonso was named the Viceroy of Portuguese India. Capturing the Indian port city of Goa after a difficult battle against a far more numerous entrenched enemy, he built Goa into Portugal's political and military center in Asia. He was renowned not just for his conquests, but also for his administration, implementing land, tax, and religious reforms in the territory he conquered.
After solidifying control over the Indian Ocean, he turned his attention further east. His capture of Malacca gave Portugal control over the extremely important Strait of Malacca, allowing them to prevent any of their rivals in the west from accessing the incredibly-lucrative maritime trade with East Asia. Though his later expedition to contain the power of the rising Mamluks ultimately failed, his fleet penetrated deeper into the Red Sea than any European navy before him.
Though the actual size of Albuquerque's conquests may seem small compared to the later European colonial empires, his capture and fortification of key ports turned Portugal into the most dominant thalassocracy in Europe. With his strategic foresight and administrative acumen, he set the model for maritime power in the east. For centuries to come, rival empires would fight viciously to reestablish the control he held over the eastern seas.

Achilles
Hector8
Achilles is one of the most famous heroes of Greek mythology and the primary character in Homer's "Iliad". He was born to the nymph Thetis
daughter of the sea god Nereus
and Peleus, mortal king of the Myrmidons of Phthia. Achilles' divine heritage endowed him with immense strength, but his human half left him mortal and vulnerable. To protect her beloved son, Thetis dipped the infant Achilles into the River Styx, rendering him invulnerable to physical harm. When doing so, however, she held him by his heel, leaving it the only part of his body unprotected.
According to later legends, when Agamemnon called the Achaeans to war, Thetis hid her son on the Aegean island of Skyros, hoping to keep him from the bloodshed. Sent to recruit Achilles, the clever King Odysseus used a ruse to draw the demigod out of hiding. Once discovered, Achilles was compelled to join the war effort.
In the early engagements of the Trojan War, Achilles quickly proved himself one of the Greek coalition's mightiest warriors, earning respect, glory, and loot. A wroth and temperamental man, Achilles soon came into conflict with the equally-prideful Agamemnon. When the latter took away his slave Briseis, an enraged Achilles refused to fight in any more battles.
Achilles was replaced as the commander of the Myrmidon host by Patroclus, his dear companion (their exact relationship is debated). Though he fought bravely, Patroclus was slain by Hector, the crown prince and mightiest warrior of Troy. Bereaved and enraged, Achilles finally rejoined the fray, defeating and killing Hector in a climactic duel. Still furious, Achilles desecrated Hector's body (a grave affront to the gods) and initially refused to return the prince's body to his father.
Towards the end of the Trojan War, Achilles' wrath would spell his doom. Paris, younger brother of the slain Hector, fired an arrow at Achilles. Angered by the killing and dishonorable treatment of Hector (one of his favorites), Apollo guided Paris' arrow to strike Achilles' heel, killing the legendary hero.
The character of Achilles is the epitome of the flawed, tragic hero
representing the moral complexity that has made Greek mythology so compelling to audiences throughout the ages. A tangled mix of strength, weakness, valor, and dishonor, Achilles remains an iconic figure in the Western literary canon.
Eldest son of King Priam of Troy, Prince Hector was Troy's mightiest warrior and its primary defender during the Trojan War.
Though a "mere mortal" in a story of gods and demigods, Hector demonstrated all the qualities of an ideal prince
brave, dutiful, honorable (most of the time), and unceasingly loyal to his people. When his younger brother Paris eloped with Queen Helen of Sparta, Hector began preparing to defend the city from the coming reprisal. An outstanding commander and leader, Hector gathered all of Troy's allies into a force that could rival the Achaean coalition.
Hector's martial prowess was equally impressive, thanks in no small part to the patronage of Apollo, who considered the prince one of his favorites. In the face of Greek's greatest heroes, the prince demonstrated his valor and might, slaying mighty warriors like Patroclus and Protesilaus. Reputedly a peace-loving family man by nature, Hector's ferocity on the battlefield exemplified his tragic sense of duty and honor.
In the face of a raging Achilles seeking vengeance for the death of Patroclus, Hector fled, his bravery briefly failing him. Despite this moment of weakness, however, the prince showed no hesitation when he was ultimately forced to duel Achilles. Even with the knowledge of Achilles' invulnerability, Hector heroically threw himself at the demigod with one final, symbolic charge. His last act was to prophesize Achilles' death at the hands of Paris and Apollo, which would not long after come true.
Hector's death spelled the beginning of the end for Troy, now deprived of its greatest warrior. Rather than a joyous occasion, as one might expect of a Greek author writing about the death of a mortal enemy of the Greeks, Homer's Iliad treats Hector's death and funeral as a somber event
the tragic finale to a tragic tale.
Though not as famous as his rival, Hector has remained an icon of honor and valor, the moral foil to the mighty-but-flawed Achilles. In the late middle ages, he was included among the ranks of the Nine Worthies, nine historical and mythical heroes considered to be the personifications of medieval chivalry (Achilles is notably not among their number).

Casimir III
Pepin III
Wielki Kr
Le Bref
Casimir III (1310-1370) was King of Poland from 1333 to 1370. Though he would be the last king of the Piast line, he earned the nickname "the Great" for his wisdom, generosity, and diplomacy. 
Son of Ladislaus I "the Elbow-High", Casimir inherited a weakened and fractured kingdom, threatened on all sides by powerful enemies. Through both skillful diplomacy and warfare, he managed to secure important allies, negotiate a peace with his Bohemian and Teutonic rivals, and add Red Ruthenia to the kingdom's territory.
Throughout his reign, Casimir greatly reformed the Polish legal code, resurrected the struggling economy, and worked to unite the disparate regions of Poland. He built over 50 castles, including the famed Trail of the Eagles' Nests, and founded several towns. For the latter, he adopted the German "Magdeburg law", granting Poland's towns the right of self-governance.
Nicknamed "the Peasants' King," Casimir was an ardent champion of the common people. His Wi
lica Statutes created a unified legal code for Poland that, while not universally adopted, protected the rights of peasants. He also placed great importance in education, founding the University of Krakow (nowadays Jagiellon University), Poland's first university and one of the oldest in Europe.
Casimir the Great passed away in 1370, one of the greatest and most beloved kings in Polish history. He left behind a wealthy and powerful kingdom, laying the groundwork for the mighty Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth soon to come.
Pepin III (714-768) was King of the Franks and the founder of the Carolingian dynasty. Though he is perhaps best known as the son of Charles Martel and the father of Charlemagne, Pepin "the Short" was an impressive figure in his own right, finishing the work of the former and laying the groundwork for the latter.
In 741, Pepin succeeded his father as Mayor of the Palace of Neustria. Though technically in the "service" of the Merovingian king Childeric III, the Mayors of the Palace were the true power behind the Frankish throne. 
In 747, Pepin also became the Mayor of the Palace for Austrasia, uniting the two mighty offices much like his father had. Unlike his father, however, Pepin saw no reason to continue the charade of Merovingian kingship.
In 751, with the blessing of the Pope, Pepin deposed Childeric and declared himself King of the Franks. The Pope himself came to Paris to anoint Pepin as king (the first time the Pope had coronated a secular ruler), granting Pepin major legitimacy and beginning the fruitful partnership between the Carolingians and the Papacy.
In 754, Pepin repaid the favor, marching on Northern Italy to defend the Pope from the Kingdom of the Lombards. He defeated the Lombards in 756, carving out a large swath of land from Rome to Ravenna for the Pope to rule personally. The "Donation of Pepin" marked the foundation of the Papal States, forever changing the shape of Italian politics.
Internally, Pepin worked tirelessly to expand the power of the Frankish king, introducing legal and ecclesiastical reforms, putting down revolts, and removing threats to his reign. He died in 768, dividing his kingdom between his sons Charlemagne and Carloman. Though not as well-known as his famous relatives, Pepin was still the founder of Europe's most powerful dynasty. His effort to build a relationship with the Papacy and the relatively stable kingdom he left behind were essential in the expansion of Francia and the Carolingian Renaissance.
