David IV
Gang Gamchanx
King David IV of Georgia (1073-1125) is one of the most important figures in Georgian history, renowned for his military achievements and for bringing his kingdom from the brink of oblivion to the heights of glory. His reign was characterized by widespread domestic reform, a massive expansion of the kingdom's borders, a religious/cultural renaissance, and the creation of a powerful central state.
After his incompetent, dithering father was deposed, the young David inherited a realm on the verge of collapse. Georgia had become a tributary of the potent Seljuk Empire, who had occupied or devastated most of the kingdom. Many of Georgia's vassals were threatening to break away, with some already having done so.
From the outset, David provided an able ruler. His first order of business was to rein in the Georgian nobility, most notably the powerful but disloyal Baghvash family. With newfound wealth coming from dispossessed rebels, David created a salaried, professional army, which he then used to drive Seljuk raiders from his land and restore economic productivity.
In 1110, David renewed the war with the Seljuk Empire, winning victory after victory and recapturing all of Georgia's lost land (along with a few new additions). His campaign culminated in 1121 at the Battle of Didgori, where his 55,000 men delivered a crushing defeat to a much larger Seljuk army thanks to superior tactics and maneuvering. After his subsequent recapture of Tbilisi (which he made the new capital) and his conquest of Shirvan, Georgia's territory spanned from the Black Sea to the Caspian.
David was not just a conqueror
he was also an energetic reformer. The new courts he established expanded royal justice to even the most marginalized of his subjects, and he practiced tolerance towards Kipchak immigrants and the Armenian Apostolic Christians recently added to his kingdom. Through new ecclesiastical reforms and restrictions, he purged the increasingly-wayward Georgian Orthodox Church of nepotism and corruption. Along with many castles and churches, he established the Gelati Monastery in Kutaisi, which became the kingdom's greatest center of art and culture.
Later generations would refer to David IV as "the Builder" for his work rebuilding the war-ravaged Kingdom of Georgia. His reign marks the start of the Golden Age of Georgia, and the kingdom he left behind would dominate the Caucasus for over a century.
Gang Gamchan (948-1031) was a renowned general and statesman of Korea's Goryeo dynasty, remembered for his mastery of both civil and martial affairs.
As legend has it, on the day Gang Gamchan was born, a meteor shot through the sky towards his house. His birthplace would later be renamed to Nakseongdae ("where the star fell"). In 983, Gang Gamchan placed first in the civil service examination, officially entering government service.
In 1009, responding to political instability in Goryeo, the Khitan Liao dynasty of Northern China invaded the kingdom, starting the second Khitan-Goryeo war. Though a relatively minor official at the time, Gang managed to convince King Hyeonjong of Goryeo to escape the palace at Gaeju and retreat south to Naju, where they would wait for a chance to strike. Due to overstretched supply lines and a zealous insurgency campaign from Goryeo's forces, the Liao army was forced to withdraw. As a result of his service during the war, Gang received several promotions and important posts.
In 1018, the Liao returned with another invasion force, and Gang Gamchan became one of the few voices urging the king to resist the invaders. Gang was tapped to serve as supreme commander of the Goryeo forces for the duration of the war. Despite being over 70 years old by this point, he proved to be a clever and resourceful commander. At Heunghwajin, Gang opened up a dammed stream to flood the Liao army, then annihilated the survivors at the Battle of Gwiju.
For his victory, Gang Gamchan became a national hero and was credited as the savior of the nation. In 1974, the Anguksa shrine was built at Nakseongdae, where every year a ceremony is held to honor the legendary general.

Sun Tzu
Artist of War
Sun Tzu, courtesy name Changqing, was born in the Kingdom of Qi during the late Spring and Autumn period. With Wu Zixu's recommendation, he later earned the favor of the Wu King Helu after presenting his writings in "The Art of War". Utilizing a variety of alternating strategies in the battle of Boju, he directed the Wu army to continuously defeat Chu forces, making their way into the Chu capital and forcing the king to flee. Through this battle, Sun Tzu became more than just a writer of military strategy, but a practitioner 
 renowned teachings were no longer mere words on a page, but entire methodologies of practical governance that allowed the Wu to survive against greater surrounding powers. While the Wu army later retreated due to interference from the Qin and Yue, Sun Tzu employed learnings from his victories to significantly improve his abilities. His writings became a timeless classic, and he himself is revered as a master of military strategy with long-lasting influence.

Ivar
The Boneless
Ivar the Boneless was a Viking commander active in the British Isles and Ireland in the ninth century, claimed by sagas to be the son of Ragnar Lodbrok. In 865 CE, he led a coalition of armies in an invasion of East Anglia, spending the winter there before moving on to raid York in 866. In 867, he inflicted a heavy defeat upon the King of Northumbria, setting up camp in Nottingham for the winter and intermittently waging war against Mercia and Wessex, exacting tribute and free passage through sieges and negotiation. In 869, he invaded East Anglia again, where he executed King Edmund. After the assault he took his forces across the Irish Sea, establishing a stronghold in between Britain and Ireland and making Dublin one of the centers of his power.
In 870 he laid siege to Alt Clut (Dumbarton Rock), returning to Dublin in 871 with plentiful captives and loot. He appears infrequently in subsequent English records, his figure and deeds scattered to the winds like the rubble of the cities he pillaged.

Alp Arslan
Sultan of SeljukK
Alp Arslan became sultan of the Seljuk empire in 1063 at the age of 33. Under his reign, the empire reached its peak in military and cultural fronts, marking the beginning of a new era in the middle east.
Alp Arslan's name, which meant "heroic lion" in Turkic, was befitting of his stature as a great conqueror. During his 1071 campaign against the Byzantine Empire, Alp Arslan won the decisive Battle of Manzikert, where he used a crescent formation and lured the Byzantines into a punishing ambush. Even the emperor Romanos IV was captured, though he was later released in what has now become a highly popularized tale. Byzantine defense on the eastern front heavily suffered from this defeat, and the battle decidedly shifted the balance in Anatolia in favor of Seljuk ascendancy.
Outside of military campaigns, Alp Arslan also pushed for administrative and academic advancements. He supported the construction of several higher institutions and was known as a patron of scholars and artists. For his many contributions, his reign is recognized as belonging to a period of the Islamic Golden Age.

Hayam Wuruk
The Thalassocrat
Hayam Wuruk was born into Majapahit royalty and inherited the throne in 1350 as a young 16-year old emperor. Together with prime minister Gajah Mada, they ruled the empire at the height of its power. Making massive strides in political and economic influence, Hayam Wuruk led Majapahit to become one of the greatest powers in Southeast Asia.
During his reign, the Majapahit empire launched wars against Sumatra and Bali and, along with establishing tributaries and alliances, these campaigns effectively expanded Majapahit territory until the mighty thalassocracy dominated the entire Malacca Strait spice trade. Working closely with Gajah Mada, Hayam Wuruk also encouraged developments in commerce, culture, and religious exchange. In his personal life, Hayam Wuruk was known for his penchant for grandeur as a patron of the arts and devout practitioner of religion; much of the ceremonial rites and royal practices of his court are recorded in detail in the Nagarakretagama, the famed Old Javanese eulogy.
After Hayam Wuruk's death in 1389, a civil war over the right to succession erupted and led to the gradual decline of the great Majapahit empire.

Pelagius
Unruly Revivalist
Pelagius was a Visigothic nobleman who founded the Kingdom of Asturias, ruling it from 718 until his death in 737. Through his victory in the Battle of Covadonga, he is credited with beginning the Reconquista, the Christian reconquest of the Iberian peninsula from the Moors. He established an independent Christian state in opposition to Moorish hegemony. The chief sources for Pelagius's life and career are two Latin chronicles written in the late ninth century from Asturias. The former is the Chronica Albeldensia, written at Albelda towards 881, and the latter is Chronicle of Alfonso III, which was revised in the early tenth century.

Vercingetorix
King of GaulN
Vercingetorix was a Gallic chieftain of the Arverni tribe as well as a prominent military leader in the history of Gaul. In the latter period of the Gallic Wars, he united several Gallic tribes in an armed coalition against Roman expansion.
Vercingetorix (b. 82 BC) was elected as leader of the Gallic coalition in 52 BC and initiated a large-scale revolt against Julius Caesar. In the beginning of the battle, he heavily utilized barricade and resource depletion strategies to effectively cut off Roman supplies. In fall of the same year, the army was forced to retreat in the Battle of Alesia after being surrounded by Caesar's troops. Unable to make a breakthrough or call for successful aid, Vercingetorix eventually surrendered to the Romans and, after being taken prisoner to Rome, was executed after Caesar's triumph in 46 BC.
Vercingetorix was the first political and military leader to have united major Gallic tribes in history. Since the 19th century, he is increasingly revered as a national hero of France 
 an important cultural symbol of French resistance against foreign aggression.

Elizabeth I
Queen Gloriana
Elizabeth I was the fifth and last monarch of the Tudors. Her reign as Queen of England and Ireland from 1558 to 1603 marked an important period of English social change and national growth, and is thus known as the Elizabethan era.
Elizabeth I was born in 1533 as the daughter of Henry VIII and Anne Boleyn. During the rule of her half sister Mary I, Elizabeth was briefly imprisoned on suspicion of supporting Protestant rebels. After ascending to the throne in 1558 following the death of Mary, Elizabeth issued a series of arrangements 
 including the Act of Supremacy and the Act of Uniformity 
 referred to as the Elizabethan Religious Settlement. These acts helped establish an English church that relieved the religious division in England at the time. Never married, she frequently would use her hand in marriage as political bargain, maneuvering between the European powers of France and Spain in effort to maintain English sovereignty and security. After her death in 1603, Elizabeth was succeeded by her first cousin twice removed, James VI of Scotland, which effectively marked the end of the Tudor dynasty.
The reign of Elizabeth I offered more than 40 years of relative stability for England. During this period, English literature and drama flourished alongside maritime exploration; lending full support to privateers and expeditioners such as Francis Drake, she laid the foundations for England's premodern naval operations and global commerce network.

Yahya ibn Khalid
Grand Vizier
Yahya ibn Khalid (c. 740-805) was one of the most influential political figures during the height of the Abbasid Dynasty. A key member of the Barmakid family, who were originally Buddhist leaders from Balkh in modern day northern Afghanistan, he rose to great power under the Abbasids after Islamic conquest.
Having entered the royal court at a young age, Yahya became tutor and advisor to prince Harun. After Harun claimed his title as Harun al-Rashid in 786, he named Yahya grand vizier with full authority over all branches of government. With immense powers vested in the vizierate, Yahya was even reportedly given the caliphal seal. Along with his sons al-Fadl and Ja'far, Yahya governed with diligence and was known for his patronage of scholars. Expending massive efforts to facilitate the translation of various Greek and Indian sources to Arabic, he was a major contributor to the centuries-long Translation Movement and considered a founding figure of the Islamic Golden Age.
By 803, the Barmakids had lost the favor of Harun al-Rashid; the family fell into disgrace, Ja'far was executed, and Yahya was imprisoned along with al-Fadl. Following the death of Yahya ibn Khalid in prison in 805, the glorious reign of the Barmakids thus ended.
